- The local part (before the '@' symbol) can include letters (both uppercase and lowercase), digits, dots ('.'), underscores ('_'), percent signs ('%'), plus signs ('+'), and hyphens ('-').
- The domain part (after the '@' symbol) can include letters, digits, dots, and hyphens.
- The top-level domain (TLD) must consist of at least two letters.
- The domain can optionally include a country-specific TLD, which also consists of at least two letters.
- The domain must not start or end with a hyphen, and it must not contain consecutive dots.

Write a function `is_valid_email(email)` that returns `True` if the email address is valid according to the above rules, and `False` otherwise.

**Sample Input:**
```
user@example.com
user.name+tag+sorting@example.com
user-name@example.co.uk

user-name@sub-domain.example.fr
user@ex-ample.com

@domain.com

True
True
True
True
False
False
False
False

False

False
False
False
False

```

```python
import re

# Regular expression for matching valid email addresses
email_regex = re.compile(
    r'^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+'          # Local part
    r'@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+'             # '@' symbol followed by domain name
    r'\.[a-zA-Z]{2,}'              # Top-level domain
    r'(\.[a-zA-Z]{2,})?$'           # Optional country-specific domain
)

def is_valid_email(email):
    """
    Validates an email address based on specific rules.

    Parameters:
    email (str): The email address to validate.

    Returns:
    bool: True if the email address is valid, False otherwise.
    """
    return email_regex.match(email) is not None

# Example usage:
test_emails = [
    "user@example.com",
    "user.name+tag+sorting@example.com",
    "user-name@example.co.uk",
    "user_name@example.org",
    "user-name@sub-domain.example.fr",
    "user@ex-ample.com",
    "user@example.c",
    "user@.com",
    "user@.com.",
    "user@domain..com",
    "user@domain.c0m",
    "user@domain.-com",
    "user@domain.com.",
    "user@-domain.com",
    "@domain.com",
    "user@",
    "user@domain",
    "user@domain.c0m",
    "user@domain.toolongtld",
    "user.name+tag+sorting@."
]

for email in test_emails:
    print(f'{email}: {is_valid_email(email)}')
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that reads a JSON configuration file and updates a specified key with a new value. The script should accept three command-line arguments: the key to update, the new value, and the filename of the JSON file. The script must handle errors gracefully, such as missing files or invalid JSON format, and ensure that the file is saved correctly.

**Input:**
- The script is executed from the command line with three arguments:
  1. `<key>`: The key in the JSON file to be updated.
  2. `<new_value>`: The new value to assign to the key.
  3. `<filename>`: The name of the JSON file to be updated.

**Output:**
- If the key exists in the JSON file, it should be updated with the new value, and a confirmation message should be printed.
- If the key does not exist, it should be added to the JSON file with the new value, and a warning message should be printed.
- If the file does not exist, an error message should be printed.
- If the file is not a valid JSON file, an error message should be printed.
- If any other unexpected error occurs, an error message should be printed.

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py "username" "newuser" "config.json"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Updated key 'username' to 'newuser' in config.json.
```

```python
import json
import sys

def update_json_config(key, new_value, filename='config.json'):
    """
    Updates a key-value pair in a JSON configuration file.

    Parameters:
    key (str): The key to update in the JSON file.
    new_value (str): The new value to assign to the key.
    filename (str): The name of the JSON file to update.

    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        with open(filename, 'r') as file:
            config_data = json.load(file)

        if key in config_data:
            config_data[key] = new_value
        else:
            print(f"Warning: Key '{key}' not found in the JSON file. Adding new key with the value.")
            config_data[key] = new_value

        with open(filename, 'w') as file:
            json.dump(config_data, file, indent=4)

        print(f"Updated key '{key}' to '{new_value}' in {filename}.")

    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {filename} does not exist.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"Error: The file {filename} is not a valid JSON file.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 4:
        print("Usage: python script.py <key> <new_value> <filename>")
    else:
        key_to_update = sys.argv[1]
        new_value = sys.argv[2]
        file_name = sys.argv[3]
        update_json_config(key_to_update, new_value, file_name)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that automates the retrieval of weather data from the OpenWeatherMap API and stores the data in a CSV file. Your script should handle network errors gracefully and only save data if the API request is successful. The CSV file should include a header row with the following columns: Date, City, Temperature (C), and Weather Description. The date should be formatted as `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS`.

**Input:**
- The API key for OpenWeatherMap.
- The name of the city for which to fetch the weather data.

**Output:**
- A CSV file named `weather_data.csv` containing the weather data.
- Print "Weather data saved successfully." if the data is saved successfully.
- Print an error message if the API request fails or if there is a network issue.

**Sample Input:**
- API Key: `your_api_key_here`
- City Name: `London`

**Sample Output:**
- `weather_data.csv` with the following content:
  ```
  Date,City,Temperature (C),Weather Description
  2023-10-05 14:30:00,London,15.2,clear sky
  ```
- Console Output:
  ```
  Weather data saved successfully.
  ```

```python
import requests
import csv
from datetime import datetime

def fetch_weather_data(api_key, city_name):
    """
    Fetches weather data from the OpenWeatherMap API for a given city.

    Parameters:
    api_key (str): The API key for OpenWeatherMap.
    city_name (str): The name of the city for which to fetch the weather data.

    Returns:
    dict: The JSON response from the API.
    """
    base_url = "http://api.openweathermap.org/data/2.5/weather?"
    complete_url = f"{base_url}appid={api_key}&q={city_name}&units=metric"
    response = requests.get(complete_url)
    return response.json()

def save_to_csv(weather_data, filename='weather_data.csv'):
    """
    Saves the weather data to a CSV file.

    Parameters:
    weather_data (dict): The weather data to save.
    filename (str): The name of the CSV file to save the data to.
    """
    with open(filename, mode='a', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
        writer = csv.writer(file)

        # Write the header if the file is empty
        if file.tell() == 0:
            writer.writerow(['Date', 'City', 'Temperature (C)', 'Weather Description'])

        # Write the weather data
        date = datetime.now().strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S')
        city = weather_data['name']
        temperature = weather_data['main']['temp']
        weather_description = weather_data['weather'][0]['description']
        row = [date, city, temperature, weather_description]
        writer.writerow(row)

def main():
    """
    Main function to fetch weather data and save it to a CSV file.
    """
    api_key = '<YOUR_API_KEY>'
    city_name = 'London'  # You can change the city name here
    
    try:
        weather_data = fetch_weather_data(api_key, city_name)
        if weather_data.get('cod') != 200:
            print(f"Error: {weather_data.get('message')}")
        else:
            save_to_csv(weather_data)
            print("Weather data saved successfully.")
    except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
        print(f"Network error: {e}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, id, department, salary):
        """
        Initializes the Employee object with the given attributes.
        
        :param name: str, the name of the employee
        :param id: int, the ID of the employee
        :param department: str, the department where the employee works
        :param salary: float, the salary of the employee
        """

        self.id = id
        self.department = department
        self.salary = salary

        """
        Returns a formatted string containing the employee's information.
        
        :return: str, formatted employee information
        """
        return f"Employee Name: {self.name}, ID: {self.id}, Department: {self.department}, Salary: ${self.salary:.2f}"
    
    def give_raise(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the employee's salary by a specified positive amount.
        
        :param amount: float, the amount to raise the salary by
        :return: str, message indicating the raise and new salary or error message
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.salary += amount
            return f"${amount} raise given to {self.name}. New salary: ${self.salary:.2f}"
        else:
            return "Raise amount must be positive."

        """
        Changes the employee's department to a specified department.
        
        :param new_department: str, the new department for the employee
        :return: str, message indicating the promotion
        """
        self.department = new_department
        return f"{self.name} has been promoted to the {new_department} department."

```python
def add(x, y):
    """Return the sum of x and y."""
    return x + y

def subtract(x, y):
    """Return the difference of x and y."""
    return x - y

def multiply(x, y):
    """Return the product of x and y."""
    return x * y

def divide(x, y):
    """Return the quotient of x and y, or an error message if y is zero."""
    if y == 0:
        return "Error! Division by zero."
    else:
        return x / y

def calculator():
    """Run a simple command-line calculator that performs basic arithmetic operations."""
    while True:
        print("\nSimple Calculator")
        print("Available operations:")
        print("1. Add")
        print("2. Subtract")
        print("3. Multiply")
        print("4. Divide")
        print("5. Exit")

        choice = input("Enter choice (1/2/3/4/5): ")

        if choice == '5':
            print("Exiting the calculator.")
            break

        if choice not in ('1', '2', '3', '4'):
            print("Invalid choice. Please select a valid operation.")
            continue

        try:
            num1 = float(input("Enter first number: "))
            num2 = float(input("Enter second number: "))
        except ValueError:
            print("Invalid input. Please enter numeric values.")
            continue

        if choice == '1':
            print(f"{num1} + {num2} = {add(num1, num2)}")

        elif choice == '2':
            print(f"{num1} - {num2} = {subtract(num1, num2)}")

        elif choice == '3':
            print(f"{num1} * {num2} = {multiply(num1, num2)}")

        elif choice == '4':
            result = divide(num1, num2)
            print(f"{num1} / {num2} = {result}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculator()

- **Create an account**: Initialize an account with an owner's name and an optional initial balance.
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The withdrawal amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.
- **Display Balance**: Print the current balance and the account owner's name.

Your program should handle multiple operations on a single account. For simplicity, assume all inputs are valid and formatted as specified.

**Input:**
- The first line contains the owner's name and an optional initial balance (default is 0.0).
- The subsequent lines contain operations in the format:
  - `deposit <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount.
  - `withdraw <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount.
  - `balance`: Display the current balance and account owner's name.

**Output:**
- For each `deposit` and `withdraw` operation, print a confirmation message.
- For each `balance` operation, print the account owner's name and the current balance.

**Sample Input:**
```
John Doe 100.0
deposit 50
withdraw 30
balance
withdraw 150
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Added 50 to the balance.
Withdrew 30 from the balance.
Account owner: John Doe

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a bank account with an owner and an initial balance.
        
        :param owner: The name of the account owner
        :param balance: The initial balance of the account, default is 0.0
        """
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit money into the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit, must be positive
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Added {amount} to the balance.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw money from the bank account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw, must be positive and less than or equal to the current balance
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount} from the balance.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Display the current balance of the bank account.
        """
        print(f"Account owner: {self.owner}")
        print(f"Account balance: {self.balance}")

```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `DatabaseConnection` that manages SQLite database operations. The class should support connecting to a database, executing SQL queries, and closing the connection. The class must handle exceptions and log errors appropriately. Your implementation should include methods for connecting to the database, executing queries, and closing the connection. 

**Methods:**
- `connect()`: Establishes a connection to the SQLite database.
- `execute_query(query, params=None)`: Executes a given SQL query with optional parameters and returns the result.
- `close()`: Closes the database connection.

**Logging Requirements:**
- Use Python's `logging` module to log informational and error messages.
- Log errors with a level of `ERROR` and include the error message.
- Log successful operations with a level of `INFO`.

**Sample Usage:**
```python
db = DatabaseConnection('test.db')
db.connect()
db.execute_query("CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT)")
db.execute_query("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", ("Alice",))
users = db.execute_query("SELECT * FROM users")
print(users)
db.close()
```

**Expected Output:**
```
[(1, 'Alice')]
```

```python
import sqlite3
import logging

class DatabaseConnection:
    def __init__(self, db_name='example.db'):
        """
        Initializes the DatabaseConnection with the specified database name.
        
        :param db_name: Name of the SQLite database file.
        """
        self.db_name = db_name
        self.connection = None
        self.logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
        logging.basicConfig(level=logging.ERROR, format='%(asctime)s - %(levelname)s - %(message)s')

    def connect(self):
        """
        Establishes a connection to the SQLite database.
        
        Raises:
            sqlite3.Error: If the connection fails.
        """
        try:
            self.connection = sqlite3.connect(self.db_name)
            self.logger.info(f"Connected to the database {self.db_name} successfully.")
        except sqlite3.Error as e:
            self.logger.error(f"Failed to connect to the database {self.db_name}. Error: {e}")
            raise

    def execute_query(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Executes a given SQL query with optional parameters and returns the result.
        
        :param query: SQL query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the SQL query.
        :return: Result of the query execution.
        :raises sqlite3.Error: If the query execution fails.
        """
        try:
            cursor = self.connection.cursor()
            if params:
                cursor.execute(query, params)
            else:
                cursor.execute(query)
            self.connection.commit()
            self.logger.info("Query executed successfully.")
            return cursor.fetchall()
        except sqlite3.Error as e:
            self.logger.error(f"Failed to execute query. Error: {e}")
            raise

    def close(self):
        """
        Closes the database connection.
        
        Raises:
            sqlite3.Error: If the connection cannot be closed.
        """
        try:
            if self.connection:
                self.connection.close()
                self.logger.info(f"Connection to the database {self.db_name} closed.")
        except sqlite3.Error as e:
            self.logger.error(f"Failed to close the connection to the database {self.db_name}. Error: {e}")
            raise
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script to process a CSV file containing customer data. The CSV file includes the following columns: `customer_id`, `name`, `email`, and `subscription_status`. The `subscription_status` can either be "active" or "inactive". Your script should output the number of active and inactive subscribers. The script must handle potential errors such as missing files or incorrect data formats.

**Input:**
- A CSV file path as a string.
- The CSV file contains the columns `customer_id`, `name`, `email`, and `subscription_status`.

**Output:**
- Print the number of active subscribers.
- Print the number of inactive subscribers.

**Sample Input:**
```
customers.csv
```
Contents of `customers.csv`:
```
customer_id,name,email,subscription_status
1,John Doe,john.doe@example.com,active
2,Jane Smith,jane.smith@example.com,inactive
3,Bob Johnson,bob.johnson@example.com,active
4,Alice Brown,alice.brown@example.com,inactive
5,Charlie Davis,charlie.davis@example.com,active
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Number of active subscribers: 3
Number of inactive subscribers: 2
```

```python
import csv
from collections import defaultdict

def count_subscribers(csv_file_path):
    """
    Reads a CSV file and counts the number of active and inactive subscribers.
    
    Parameters:
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the CSV file containing customer data.
    
    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary with keys 'active' and 'inactive' representing the counts of each subscription status.
    """
    subscriber_counts = defaultdict(int)
    
    try:
        with open(csv_file_path, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            csv_reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            
            if not all(field in csv_reader.fieldnames for field in ['customer_id', 'name', 'email', 'subscription_status']):
                raise ValueError("CSV file does not have the required columns.")
            
            for row in csv_reader:
                subscription_status = row['subscription_status'].strip().lower()
                
                if subscription_status in ['active', 'inactive']:
                    subscriber_counts[subscription_status] += 1
                else:
                    print(f"Warning: Unexpected subscription status '{row['subscription_status']}' for customer {row['customer_id']}. Skipping.")
                    continue
        
        return subscriber_counts
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {csv_file_path} was not found.")
        return None
    except IOError:
        print(f"Error: Failed to read the file {csv_file_path}.")
        return None
    except ValueError as ve:
        print(f"Error: {ve}")
        return None
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")
        return None

# Example usage
csv_file_path = 'customers.csv'
subscriber_counts = count_subscribers(csv_file_path)

if subscriber_counts:
    print(f"Number of active subscribers: {subscriber_counts['active']}")
    print(f"Number of inactive subscribers: {subscriber_counts['inactive']}")
```<#sep#>You are given a task to write a Python script that reads a CSV file and computes the average of a specified column. Your script must handle command-line arguments using the `argparse` module. The script should output the average of the specified column rounded to two decimal places. If the specified column does not exist or contains no valid numbers, the script should output an appropriate error message and terminate.

**Input:**
- The script will be run from the command line with two arguments:
  1. The path to the CSV file.
  2. The name of the column to average.

**Output:**
- The average of the specified column, rounded to two decimal places.
- An error message if the column does not exist or contains no valid numbers.
- An error message if the file is not found.

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py data.csv Age
```
**Sample Output:**
```
The average of column 'Age' is 30.50
```

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py data.csv Salary
```
**Sample Output:**
```
Error: Column 'Salary' does not exist in the CSV file.
```

```python
import argparse
import csv

def main():
    """
    Main function to parse command-line arguments, read the CSV file, and calculate the average of a specified column.
    """
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description='Read a CSV file and calculate the average of a specified column.')
    parser.add_argument('csv_file', help='Path to the CSV file')
    parser.add_argument('column_name', help='The name of the column to average')

    args = parser.parse_args()

    try:
        with open(args.csv_file, mode='r', newline='') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            if args.column_name not in reader.fieldnames:
                print(f"Error: Column '{args.column_name}' does not exist in the CSV file.")
                return

            total = 0
            count = 0
            for row in reader:
                try:
                    value = float(row[args.column_name])
                    total += value
                    count += 1
                except ValueError:
                    continue

            if count == 0:
                print(f"Error: No valid numbers found in column '{args.column_name}'.")
            else:
                average = total / count
                print(f"The average of column '{args.column_name}' is {average:.2f}")
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File '{args.csv_file}' not found.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```<#sep#>You are given a list of software design patterns. Your task is to classify these patterns into three categories: Creational, Structural, and Behavioral. For each pattern, provide a brief description of its purpose and classification. 

**Input:**
- A list of strings representing the names of design patterns.

**Output:**
- Print the patterns categorized into Creational, Structural, and Behavioral, along with their descriptions. Each category should be printed on a new line, followed by the patterns in that category, each on a new line with their description.

**Sample Input:**
```python
['Singleton', 'Adapter', 'Observer', 'Factory Method', 'Decorator', 'Strategy', 'Command', 'Facade', 'Builder', 'Composite']
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Creational Patterns:
  - Singleton: Ensures a class has only one instance and provides a global point of access to it.
  - Factory Method: Defines an interface for creating an object, but lets subclasses decide which class to instantiate.
  - Builder: Separate the construction of a complex object from its representation so that the same construction process can create different representations.

Structural Patterns:
  - Adapter: Converts the interface of a class into another interface clients expect.
  - Decorator: Attach additional responsibilities to an object dynamically.
  - Facade: Provide a unified interface to a set of interfaces in a subsystem. Facade defines a higher-level interface that makes the subsystem easier to use.
  - Composite: Compose objects into tree structures to represent part-whole hierarchies. Composite lets clients treat individual objects and compositions of objects uniformly.

Behavioral Patterns:
  - Observer: Defines a dependency between objects so that when one object changes state, all its dependents are notified and updated automatically.
  - Strategy: Enable a method to be swapped out at runtime by any other method (strategy) without the client realizing it.
  - Command: Encapsulate a request as an object, thereby letting you parameterize clients with different requests, queue or log requests, and support undoable operations.
```

```python
def classify_design_patterns(patterns):
    """
    Classify the given list of design patterns into Creational, Structural, and Behavioral categories.
    Print each category followed by the patterns in that category along with their descriptions.
    
    :param patterns: List of strings representing design patterns.
    """
    classifications = {
        'Singleton': ('Creational', 'Ensures a class has only one instance and provides a global point of access to it.'),
        'Adapter': ('Structural', 'Converts the interface of a class into another interface clients expect.'),
        'Observer': ('Behavioral', 'Defines a dependency between objects so that when one object changes state, all its dependents are notified and updated automatically.'),
        'Factory Method': ('Creational', 'Defines an interface for creating an object, but lets subclasses decide which class to instantiate.'),
        'Decorator': ('Structural', 'Attach additional responsibilities to an object dynamically.'),
        'Strategy': ('Behavioral', 'Enable a method to be swapped out at runtime by any other method (strategy) without the client realizing it.'),
        'Command': ('Behavioral', 'Encapsulate a request as an object, thereby letting you parameterize clients with different requests, queue or log requests, and support undoable operations.'),
        'Facade': ('Structural', 'Provide a unified interface to a set of interfaces in a subsystem. Facade defines a higher-level interface that makes the subsystem easier to use.'),
        'Builder': ('Creational', 'Separate the construction of a complex object from its representation so that the same construction process can create different representations.'),
        'Composite': ('Structural', 'Compose objects into tree structures to represent part-whole hierarchies. Composite lets clients treat individual objects and compositions of objects uniformly.')
    }
    
    categorized_patterns = {
        'Creational': [],
        'Structural': [],
        'Behavioral': []
    }

    uncategorized_patterns = []

    for pattern in patterns:
        if pattern in classifications:
            category, description = classifications[pattern]
            categorized_patterns[category].append((pattern, description))
        else:
            uncategorized_patterns.append(pattern)

    for category, listed_patterns in categorized_patterns.items():
        print(f"\n{category} Patterns:")
        for pattern, description in listed_patterns:
            print(f"  - {pattern}: {description}")

    if uncategorized_patterns:
        print("\nUncategorized Patterns:")
        for pattern in uncategorized_patterns:
            print(f"  - {pattern}")

# Sample List of patterns
patterns = ['Singleton', 'Adapter', 'Observer', 'Factory Method', 'Decorator', 'Strategy', 'Command', 'Facade', 'Builder', 'Composite']

# Classify the patterns
classify_design_patterns(patterns)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Polynomial` class in Python that can represent a polynomial and perform basic arithmetic operations: addition, subtraction, and multiplication. The constructor of the class should accept a list of coefficients, where the i-th element of the list represents the coefficient of the x^i term. Implement methods to add, subtract, and multiply two polynomials, returning a new `Polynomial` object representing the result.

**Input:**
- The input will be provided through the constructor of the `Polynomial` class as a list of integers representing the coefficients of the polynomial.

**Output:**
- The output should be a new `Polynomial` object resulting from the arithmetic operations.

**Sample Input:**
```python
p1 = Polynomial([1, 2, 3])  # Represents 1 + 2x + 3x^2
p2 = Polynomial([4, 5])     # Represents 4 + 5x
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Sum of p1 and p2: 5 + 7x + 3x^2
Difference of p1 and p2: -3 - 3x + 3x^2
Product of p1 and p2: 4 + 13x + 22x^2 + 15x^3
```

```python
class Polynomial:
    def __init__(self, coefficients):
        """
        Initialize a polynomial with a list of coefficients.
        :param coefficients: List of integers where the i-th element is the coefficient of x^i.
        """
        self.coefficients = coefficients

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Add two polynomials.
        :param other: Another Polynomial object.
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the sum.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Polynomial):
            raise ValueError("Addition is only supported between two Polynomial objects.")
        
        max_len = max(len(self.coefficients), len(other.coefficients))
        extended_self = self.coefficients + [0] * (max_len - len(self.coefficients))
        extended_other = other.coefficients + [0] * (max_len - len(other.coefficients))
        
        result = [a + b for a, b in zip(extended_self, extended_other)]
        return Polynomial(result)

    def __sub__(self, other):
        """
        Subtract two polynomials.
        :param other: Another Polynomial object.
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the difference.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Polynomial):
            raise ValueError("Subtraction is only supported between two Polynomial objects.")
        
        max_len = max(len(self.coefficients), len(other.coefficients))
        extended_self = self.coefficients + [0] * (max_len - len(self.coefficients))
        extended_other = other.coefficients + [0] * (max_len - len(other.coefficients))
        
        result = [a - b for a, b in zip(extended_self, extended_other)]
        return Polynomial(result)

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiply two polynomials.
        :param other: Another Polynomial object.
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the product.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Polynomial):
            raise ValueError("Multiplication is only supported between two Polynomial objects.")
        
        result = [0] * (len(self.coefficients) + len(other.coefficients) - 1)
        for i, a in enumerate(self.coefficients):
            for j, b in enumerate(other.coefficients):
                result[i + j] += a * b
        return Polynomial(result)

    def __repr__(self):
        """
        Return a string representation of the polynomial.
        :return: String representation of the polynomial.
        """
        terms = []
        for power, coeff in enumerate(self.coefficients):
            if coeff == 0:
                continue
            terms.append(f"{coeff}x^{power}" if power > 0 else f"{coeff}")
        return " + ".join(terms) if terms else "0"

# Example usage:
p1 = Polynomial([1, 2, 3])  # Represents 1 + 2x + 3x^2
p2 = Polynomial([4, 5])     # Represents 4 + 5x

# Adding two polynomials
sum_poly = p1 + p2
print(f"Sum of p1 and p2: {sum_poly}")

# Subtracting two polynomials
diff_poly = p1 - p2
print(f"Difference of p1 and p2: {diff_poly}")

# Multiplying two polynomials
prod_poly = p1 * p2
print(f"Product of p1 and p2: {prod_poly}")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Portfolio` class in Python that manages a collection of `Stock` objects. Each `Stock` object is characterized by a unique ticker symbol and a current price. Your `Portfolio` class should support the following operations:

- **Add Stock**: Add a `Stock` object to the portfolio. Ensure that only `Stock` objects can be added.
- **Remove Stock**: Remove a `Stock` object from the portfolio using its ticker symbol. Raise an error if the stock does not exist.
- **Get Total Value**: Calculate and return the total value of all stocks in the portfolio.

#### Sample Input/Output:
```python
# Sample Input
portfolio = Portfolio()
portfolio.add_stock(Stock("AAPL", 150.0))
portfolio.add_stock(Stock("GOOGL", 2800.0))
portfolio.remove_stock("AAPL")
total_value = portfolio.get_total_value()

# Sample Output
total_value  # Should output 2800.0
```

```python
class Stock:
    def __init__(self, ticker, price):
        """
        Initialize a Stock object with a ticker symbol and a price.

        :param ticker: A string representing the stock's ticker symbol.
        :param price: A float representing the current price of the stock.
        """
        self.ticker = ticker
        self.price = price


class Portfolio:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initialize an empty portfolio to hold Stock objects.
        """
        self.stocks = {}

    def add_stock(self, stock):
        """
        Add a Stock object to the portfolio.

        :param stock: A Stock object to be added.
        :raises ValueError: If the provided object is not a Stock instance.
        """
        if isinstance(stock, Stock):
            self.stocks[stock.ticker] = stock
        else:
            raise ValueError("Only Stock objects can be added to the portfolio")

    def remove_stock(self, ticker):
        """
        Remove a Stock object from the portfolio by its ticker symbol.

        :param ticker: A string representing the ticker symbol of the stock to be removed.
        :raises KeyError: If the stock with the given ticker symbol does not exist in the portfolio.
        """
        if ticker in self.stocks:
            del self.stocks[ticker]
        else:
            raise KeyError(f"Stock with ticker {ticker} not found in the portfolio")

    def get_total_value(self):
        """
        Calculate and return the total value of all stocks in the portfolio.

        :return: A float representing the total value of the portfolio.
        """
        return sum(stock.price for stock in self.stocks.values())
```<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a simple text-based adventure game in Python. The game world consists of four distinct locations: a forest, a cave, a beach, and a river. Each location has a unique description, a list of objects, and connections to other locations. The player can navigate between locations using commands such as "go north", "go south", "go east", and "go west". Additionally, the player can interact with objects in the current location using the command "take [object]". The game should start in the forest and continue until the player types "quit" or "exit". 

**Sample Input:**
```
go south
take torch
go east
go north
take mushroom
exit
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Welcome to the Adventure Game!
You can navigate by typing 'go [direction]' (north, south, east, west).
You can interact with objects by typing 'take [object]'.

You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.
Objects in this area: mushroom

> go south
You are inside a dark cave. It's cold and the walls are damp.
Objects in this area: torch

> take torch
You have taken the torch.

> go east
You can't go east. Please choose a valid direction.

> go north
You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.
Objects in this area: mushroom

> take mushroom
You have taken the mushroom.

> exit
Exiting game. Thanks for playing!
```

```python
# Define locations as dictionaries with descriptions, objects, and connected areas
locations = {
    'forest': {
        'description': "You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.",
        'objects': ['mushroom'],
        'connected_areas': {'south': 'cave', 'east': 'beach'}
    },
    'cave': {
        'description': "You are inside a dark cave. It's cold and the walls are damp.",
        'objects': ['torch'],
        'connected_areas': {'north': 'forest', 'west': 'river'}
    },
    'beach': {
        'description': "You are on a beautiful sandy beach with a clear blue sea.",
        'objects': ['shell'],
        'connected_areas': {'west': 'forest'}
    },
    'river': {
        'description': "You are standing next to a babbling river.",
        'objects': ['fish'],
        'connected_areas': {'east': 'cave'}
    }
}

# Start in the forest location
current_location = 'forest'

def display_location_details():
    """Display the welcome message, instructions, and current location details."""
    print("\nWelcome to the Adventure Game!")
    print("You can navigate by typing 'go [direction]' (north, south, east, west).")
    print("You can interact with objects by typing 'take [object]'.")
    print("\n" + locations[current_location]['description'])
    print("Objects in this area: " + ', '.join(locations[current_location]['objects']))

def process_command(command):
    """Process the player's command to navigate or interact with objects."""
    command_parts = command.split()
    if len(command_parts) < 2:
        print("Invalid command. At least two words required (e.g., 'go north', 'take mushroom').")
        return False

    action = command_parts[0].lower()
    object_or_direction = command_parts[1].lower()

    if action == 'go':
        if object_or_direction in locations[current_location]['connected_areas']:
            global current_location
            current_location = locations[current_location]['connected_areas'][object_or_direction]
            display_location_details()
        else:
            print(f"You can't go {object_or_direction}. Please choose a valid direction.")
    elif action == 'take':
        if object_or_direction in locations[current_location]['objects']:
            print(f"You have taken the {object_or_direction}.")
            locations[current_location]['objects'].remove(object_or_direction)
        else:
            print(f"You can't take the {object_or_direction} from here.")
    else:
        print("Invalid action. Try 'go [direction]' or 'take [object]'.")
        return False

    return True

def game_loop():
    """Main game loop to handle player input and game progression."""
    display_location_details()
    while True:
        command = input("\n> ")
        if command.lower() in ['quit', 'exit']:
            print("Exiting game. Thanks for playing!")
            break
        process_command(command)

# Start the game
if __name__ == "__main__":
    game_loop()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple genetic algorithm to evolve a population of strings to match a target string "HELLO WORLD". Your program should include the following functionalities:
- Create an initial population of random strings.
- Evaluate the fitness of each individual based on how closely it matches the target string.
- Select parents for reproduction based on their fitness scores.
- Perform crossover between pairs of parents to produce offspring.
- Introduce mutations in the offspring with a given mutation rate.
- Evolve the population over a specified number of generations and output the best individual found.

**Input:**
- No input is required for this problem.

**Output:**
- Print the generation number and the best individual string found at every 100 generations.
- If the target string "HELLO WORLD" is found, print the generation number and the string and terminate the program.

**Sample Output:**
```
Generation 0: Best fitness 2, Best individual HELLO WORLD
Generation 100: Best fitness 5, Best individual HELLO WORLD
Solved in Generation 150: HELLO WORLD
```

```python
import random
import string

target = "HELLO WORLD"
population_size = 100
mutation_rate = 0.01
generations = 1000

def create_individual(length=len(target)):
    """Create a random individual as a string of uppercase letters and spaces."""
    return ''.join(random.choice(string.ascii_uppercase + ' ') for _ in range(length))

def create_initial_population(size=population_size):
    """Create the initial population of individuals."""
    return [create_individual() for _ in range(size)]

def evaluate_fitness(individual):
    """Evaluate the fitness of an individual based on how close it is to the target string."""
    return sum(individual[i] == target[i] for i in range(len(target)))

def select_parents(population, fitnesses):
    """Select a pair of parents based on their fitness scores using roulette wheel selection."""
    total_fitness = sum(fitnesses)
    pick = random.uniform(0, total_fitness)
    current = 0
    for individual, fit in zip(population, fitnesses):
        current += fit
        if current > pick:
            return individual
    return population[-1]

def crossover(parent1, parent2):
    """Perform single point crossover between two parents to produce an offspring."""
    point = random.randint(1, len(target) - 1)
    return parent1[:point] + parent2[point:]

def mutate(individual):
    """Introduce random mutations in an individual with a given mutation rate."""
    return ''.join(
        random.choice(string.ascii_uppercase + ' ') 
        if random.random() < mutation_rate 
        else c 
        for c in individual
    )

initial_population = create_initial_population()

for generation in range(generations):
    fitnesses = [evaluate_fitness(individual) for individual in initial_population]
    new_population = []

    for _ in range(population_size // 2):
        parent1, parent2 = select_parents(initial_population, fitnesses), select_parents(initial_population, fitnesses)
        child1, child2 = crossover(parent1, parent2), crossover(parent2, parent1)
        new_population.extend([mutate(child1), mutate(child2)])

    initial_population = new_population[:]
    best_individual = max(initial_population, key=evaluate_fitness)
    best_fitness = evaluate_fitness(best_individual)
    if best_fitness == len(target):
        print(f"Solved in Generation {generation}: {best_individual}")
        break
    if generation % 100 == 0:
        print(f"Generation {generation}: Best fitness {best_fitness}, Best individual {best_individual}")
```<#sep#>You are given a list of commit messages, each represented as a string. Your task is to write a Python function `summarize_commit_messages` that processes these messages and returns a summary. The summary should include:
- The total number of commits.
- The most frequently occurring prefix in the commit messages. A prefix is defined as the substring of the commit message up to the first space character.
- A list of unique prefixes used in the commit messages.

**Input:**
- A list of strings, where each string is a commit message.

**Output:**
- A dictionary with the following keys:
  - `'num_commits'`: An integer representing the total number of commits.
  - `'most_common_prefix'`: A string representing the most common prefix. If there are multiple prefixes with the same highest frequency, return any one of them.
  - `'unique_prefixes'`: A list of unique prefixes.

**Sample Input:**
```python
commit_messages = [
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Update user interface",
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Add new feature",
    "Update user interface",
    "Refactor code"
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'num_commits': 6,
    'most_common_prefix': 'Fix',  # or 'Update'
    'unique_prefixes': ['Fix', 'Update', 'Add', 'Refactor']
}
```

```python
from collections import Counter

def summarize_commit_messages(commit_messages):
    """
    Generates a summary of commit messages including the number of commits,
    the most common prefix, and a list of unique prefixes.

    :param commit_messages: List of commit messages as strings.
    :return: Dictionary with keys 'num_commits', 'most_common_prefix', and 'unique_prefixes'.
    """
    num_commits = len(commit_messages)
    prefixes = []
    unique_prefixes = set()
    
    for message in commit_messages:
        prefix = message.split(' ')[0]
        prefixes.append(prefix)
        unique_prefixes.add(prefix)
    
    prefix_counts = Counter(prefixes)
    most_common_prefix = prefix_counts.most_common(1)[0][0] if prefix_counts else None
    
    summary = {
        'num_commits': num_commits,
        'most_common_prefix': most_common_prefix,
        'unique_prefixes': list(unique_prefixes)
    }
    
    return summary

# Test the function with the provided commit messages
commit_messages = [
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Update user interface",
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Add new feature",
    "Update user interface",
    "Refactor code"
]

summary = summarize_commit_messages(commit_messages)
print(summary)
```<#sep#>You are given a list of tuples, where each tuple consists of two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting in a 24-hour format (HH:MM). Your task is to merge overlapping or contiguous meetings and return a list of merged meetings sorted by their start times. Each meeting in the output should be represented as a tuple of strings in the same format.

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting.

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a merged meeting.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[("09:00", "10:30"), ("10:30", "11:00"), ("12:00", "13:00"), ("13:30", "14:00"), 
 ("14:00", "15:00"), ("15:00", "16:00"), ("10:00", "11:00"), ("11:00", "11:30"), 
 ("11:30", "12:00"), ("09:30", "10:00")]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[("09:00", "11:00"), ("12:00", "16:00")]
```

```python
def merge_meetings(meetings):
    """
    Merges overlapping or contiguous meetings and returns a list of merged meetings sorted by start times.
    
    :param meetings: List of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting.
    :return: List of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a merged meeting.
    """
    def time_to_minutes(time_str):
        """
        Converts time in 'HH:MM' format to minutes from the start of the day.
        
        :param time_str: String representing time in 'HH:MM' format.
        :return: Integer representing minutes from the start of the day.
        """
        hours, minutes = map(int, time_str.split(':'))
        return hours * 60 + minutes

    def minutes_to_time(minutes):
        """
        Converts minutes from the start of the day to time in 'HH:MM' format.
        
        :param minutes: Integer representing minutes from the start of the day.
        :return: String representing time in 'HH:MM' format.
        """
        hours = minutes // 60
        minutes = minutes % 60
        return f"{hours:02}:{minutes:02}"

    # Convert meeting times to minutes and sort them
    meetings_minutes = [(time_to_minutes(start), time_to_minutes(end)) for start, end in meetings]
    meetings_minutes.sort()

    # Merge meetings
    merged_meetings = []
    for current in meetings_minutes:
        if not merged_meetings or merged_meetings[-1][1] < current[0]:
            # No overlap, add the current meeting
            merged_meetings.append(current)
        else:
            # There is an overlap, merge the current meeting with the last one in merged_meetings
            last_meeting = merged_meetings.pop()
            merged_meetings.append((last_meeting[0], max(last_meeting[1], current[1])))

    # Convert merged meetings back to 'HH:MM' format
    return [(minutes_to_time(start), minutes_to_time(end)) for start, end in merged_meetings]
```<#sep#>You are given a string representing a mathematical expression in infix notation. Your task is to implement a function `evaluate_expression` that evaluates this expression and returns the result as a floating-point number. The expression consists of non-negative integers, decimal numbers, and the operators `+`, `-`, `*`, and `/`. Spaces may appear between numbers and operators. You can assume that the input expression is always valid and does not contain any invalid characters.

**Input:**
- A string `expression` (1 ≤ len(expression) ≤ 100) representing a valid mathematical expression.

**Output:**
- A floating-point number representing the result of the evaluated expression.

**Sample Input:**
```
"3 + 5 * (2 - 8)"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
-19.0
```

**Sample Input:**
```
"10 + 2 * 6.5"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
23.0
```

```python
def evaluate_expression(expression):
    """
    Evaluates a mathematical expression given in infix notation and returns the result as a floating-point number.
    
    :param expression: A string representing a valid mathematical expression.
    :return: A floating-point number representing the result of the evaluated expression.
    """
    def apply_operator(operators, values):
        operator = operators.pop()
        right = values.pop()
        left = values.pop()
        if operator == '+':
            values.append(left + right)
        elif operator == '-':
            values.append(left - right)
        elif operator == '*':
            values.append(left * right)
        elif operator == '/':
            values.append(left / right)

    def precedence(op):
        if op in ('+', '-'):
            return 1
        if op in ('*', '/'):
            return 2
        return 0

    expression = expression.replace(" ", "")
    operators = []
    values = []
    i = 0

    while i < len(expression):
        if expression[i] in '0123456789.':
            j = i
            while j < len(expression) and expression[j] in '0123456789.':
                j += 1
            values.append(float(expression[i:j]))
            i = j
        elif expression[i] == '(':
            operators.append(expression[i])
            i += 1
        elif expression[i] == ')':
            while operators and operators[-1] != '(':
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            operators.pop()  # Remove '('
            i += 1
        else:
            while (operators and precedence(operators[-1]) >= precedence(expression[i])):
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            operators.append(expression[i])
            i += 1

    while operators:
        apply_operator(operators, values)

    return values[0]
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `number_guessing_game()` that simulates a simple text-based number guessing game. The game randomly selects a number between 1 and 100, and the player has up to 10 attempts to guess the number. After each guess, the program should provide feedback indicating whether the guess is too low, too high, or correct. The player can also quit the game by entering the keyword "quit". The function should display the number of attempts made after each guess.

**Sample Interaction:**
```
Welcome to the Number Guessing Game!
I'm thinking of a number between 1 and 100. You have 10 attempts to guess it.
Type 'quit' to quit the game at any time.
Attempt 1/10. Enter your guess: 50
Your guess is too low.
Attempt 2/10. Enter your guess: 75
Your guess is too high.
Attempt 3/10. Enter your guess: 62
Congratulations! You've guessed the number in 3 attempt(s).
```

**Sample Interaction with Quit:**
```
Welcome to the Number Guessing Game!
I'm thinking of a number between 1 and 100. You have 10 attempts to guess it.
Type 'quit' to quit the game at any time.
Attempt 1/10. Enter your guess: quit
You've chosen to quit the game. Thanks for playing!
```

```python
import random

def number_guessing_game():
    """
    Simulates a number guessing game where the player tries to guess a randomly generated number between 1 and 100.
    The player has up to 10 attempts to guess the number. Feedback is provided after each guess, and the player can quit
    the game by entering the keyword 'quit'.
    """
    number_to_guess = random.randint(1, 100)
    max_attempts = 10
    attempts = 0
    quit_keyword = "quit"
    
    print("Welcome to the Number Guessing Game!")
    print(f"I'm thinking of a number between 1 and 100. You have {max_attempts} attempts to guess it.")
    print(f"Type '{quit_keyword}' to quit the game at any time.")
    
    while attempts < max_attempts:
        user_input = input(f"Attempt {attempts + 1}/{max_attempts}. Enter your guess: ")
        
        if user_input.lower() == quit_keyword:
            print("You've chosen to quit the game. Thanks for playing!")
            return
        
        try:
            guess = int(user_input)
        except ValueError:
            print("Invalid input. Please enter a valid number or 'quit' to exit.")
            continue
        
        attempts += 1
        
        if guess < 1 or guess > 100:
            print("Your guess is out of range. Please guess a number between 1 and 100.")
            continue
        
        if guess < number_to_guess:
            print("Your guess is too low.")
        elif guess > number_to_guess:
            print("Your guess is too high.")
        else:
            print(f"Congratulations! You've guessed the number in {attempts} attempt(s).")
            return
    
    print(f"Sorry, you've used all your attempts. The number was {number_to_guess}.")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a calendar management system. Your system should support the following operations:

1. **addEvent(start, end)**: Adds a new event to the calendar. The event is defined by its start and end times. If the new event does not overlap with any existing events, it should be added to the calendar and the function should return `True`. If there is a conflict with an existing event, the function should return `False`.

2. **removeEvent(start, end)**: Removes an event from the calendar. The event to be removed is defined by its start and end times. If the event is found and removed, the function should return `True`. If the event is not found, the function should return `False`.

3. **getEvents()**: Returns a list of all events currently in the calendar, sorted by their start times. Each event should be represented as a tuple `(start, end)`.

**Constraints:**
- `0 <= start < end <= 10^9`
- The number of operations (addEvent, removeEvent, getEvents) will not exceed `10^4`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input:
  ```
  calendar = MyCalendar()
  calendar.addEvent(10, 20)
  calendar.addEvent(30, 40)
  calendar.addEvent(15, 25)
  calendar.removeEvent(10, 20)
  calendar.getEvents()
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  False
  True
  True
  True
  [(30, 40)]
  ```

```python
class MyCalendar:
    def __init__(self):
        self.events = []

    def addEvent(self, start, end):
        """
        Adds a new event to the calendar if it does not conflict with existing events.
        
        :param start: Start time of the event.
        :param end: End time of the event.
        :return: True if the event was added, False if there is a conflict.
        """
        for existing_start, existing_end in self.events:
            if start < existing_end and end > existing_start:
                return False
        self.events.append((start, end))
        self.events.sort()
        return True

    def removeEvent(self, start, end):
        """
        Removes an event from the calendar if it exists.
        
        :param start: Start time of the event to be removed.
        :param end: End time of the event to be removed.
        :return: True if the event was removed, False if the event was not found.
        """
        for i, (existing_start, existing_end) in enumerate(self.events):
            if existing_start == start and existing_end == end:
                del self.events[i]
                return True
        return False

    def getEvents(self):
        """
        Returns a list of all events in the calendar, sorted by start time.
        
        :return: List of tuples representing the events.
        """
        return self.events
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `TemperatureConverter` class in Python that supports temperature conversions between Fahrenheit, Celsius, and Kelvin. The class should provide three methods: `to_fahrenheit`, `to_celsius`, and `to_kelvin`. Each method should accept two parameters: `temp` (the temperature value to convert) and `temp_scale` (a string indicating the scale of the input temperature, which can be 'F', 'C', or 'K'). If an invalid temperature scale is provided, the method should raise a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- `converter = TemperatureConverter()`
- `converter.to_fahrenheit(100, 'C')` should return `212.0`
- `converter.to_celsius(32, 'F')` should return `0.0`
- `converter.to_kelvin(0, 'C')` should return `273.15`

```python
class TemperatureConverter:
    def to_fahrenheit(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Fahrenheit.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Fahrenheit.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return temp
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return (temp * 9/5) + 32
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return (temp - 273.15) * 9/5 + 32
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")

    def to_celsius(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Celsius.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Celsius.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return (temp - 32) * 5/9
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return temp
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return temp - 273.15
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")

    def to_kelvin(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Kelvin.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Kelvin.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return (temp - 32) * 5/9 + 273.15
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return temp + 273.15
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return temp
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a RESTful API for a simple online store. The API should support the following functionalities:

1. **Retrieve a list of all products**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /products`
   - **Response**: A JSON array of product objects.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     [
       {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "price": 999.99},
       {"id": 2, "name": "Smartphone", "price": 499.99}
     ]
     ```

2. **Retrieve details for a specific product by its ID**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /product/{product_id}`
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "price": 999.99}
     ```

3. **Add a new product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `POST /products`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object representing the product.
   - **Sample Request Body**:
     ```json
     {"name": "Tablet", "price": 299.99}
     ```
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the newly added product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 3, "name": "Tablet", "price": 299.99}
     ```

4. **Update an existing product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `PUT /product/{product_id}`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object representing the updated product details.
   - **Sample Request Body**:
     ```json
     {"name": "Laptop Pro", "price": 1299.99}
     ```
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the updated product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop Pro", "price": 1299.99}
     ```

5. **Delete a product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `DELETE /product/{product_id}`
   - **Response**: A success message.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     "Product deleted"
     ```

```python
from flask import Flask, request
from flask_restful import Api, Resource

app = Flask(__name__)
api = Api(app)

# In-memory storage of products for demonstration purposes
products = {}

class Product(Resource):
    """
    Handles operations related to a specific product identified by product_id.
    """
    def get(self, product_id):
        """
        Retrieve details for a specific product by its ID.
        """
        product = products.get(product_id)
        if product:
            return product, 200
        return 'Product not found', 404

    def put(self, product_id):
        """
        Update an existing product.
        """
        if product_id not in products:
            return 'Product not found', 404
        data = request.json
        products[product_id] = data
        return products[product_id], 200

    def delete(self, product_id):
        """
        Delete a product.
        """
        if product_id not in products:
            return 'Product not found', 404
        del products[product_id]
        return 'Product deleted', 200

class ProductList(Resource):
    """
    Handles operations related to the collection of all products.
    """
    def get(self):
        """
        Retrieve a list of all products.
        """
        return list(products.values()), 200

    def post(self):
        """
        Add a new product.
        """
        data = request.json
        product_id = max(products.keys(), default=0) + 1
        products[product_id] = data
        return products[product_id], 201

api.add_resource(Product, '/product/<int:product_id>')
api.add_resource(ProductList, '/products')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a feature for a social media platform called "Trend Watch". This feature allows users to follow specific topics and receive updates when content related to those topics is posted. Implement the following functions:

- `follow_topic(user_id, topic)`: Allows a user to follow a specific topic.
- `unfollow_topic(user_id, topic)`: Allows a user to unfollow a specific topic.
- `get_updates_for_topic(topic)`: Returns a list of updates related to a specific topic that the user is subscribed to.

The updates are stored in a dictionary called `updates` where the keys are topic names and the values are lists of updates.

**Input:**
- A series of operations in the form of function calls to `follow_topic`, `unfollow_topic`, and `get_updates_for_topic`.

**Output:**
- For each call to `get_updates_for_topic`, print the list of updates for the specified topic.

**Sample Input:**
```
follow_topic(1, 'python')
follow_topic(1, 'javascript')
follow_topic(2, 'python')
get_updates_for_topic('python')
get_updates_for_topic('javascript')
unfollow_topic(1, 'javascript')
get_updates_for_topic('python')
```

**Sample Output:**
```
['New Python 3.11 Release Notes', 'Python Best Practices Tips']
['ES2022 Features Overview']
['New Python 3.11 Release Notes', 'Python Best Practices Tips']
```

```python
# Initialize the data structures
subscriptions = {}  # Maps user_id to a set of topics they follow
updates = {
    'python': ["New Python 3.11 Release Notes", "Python Best Practices Tips"],
    'javascript': ["ES2022 Features Overview"]
}  # Maps topic to a list of updates

def follow_topic(user_id, topic):
    """
    Allows a user to follow a specific topic.
    
    Parameters:
    - user_id: The ID of the user.
    - topic: The topic to follow.
    """
    if user_id not in subscriptions:
        subscriptions[user_id] = set()
    subscriptions[user_id].add(topic)

def unfollow_topic(user_id, topic):
    """
    Allows a user to unfollow a specific topic.
    
    Parameters:
    - user_id: The ID of the user.
    - topic: The topic to unfollow.
    """
    if user_id in subscriptions and topic in subscriptions[user_id]:
        subscriptions[user_id].remove(topic)
        if not subscriptions[user_id]:  # If the user is not following any topics, remove them from the dict
            del subscriptions[user_id]

def get_updates_for_topic(topic):
    """
    Returns a list of updates related to a specific topic.
    
    Parameters:
    - topic: The topic to get updates for.
    
    Returns:
    - A list of updates for the given topic.
    """
    return updates.get(topic, [])

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    follow_topic(1, 'python')
    follow_topic(1, 'javascript')
    follow_topic(2, 'python')
    print(get_updates_for_topic('python'))
    print(get_updates_for_topic('javascript'))
    unfollow_topic(1, 'javascript')
    print(get_updates_for_topic('python'))

- `get_details`: Returns a formatted string containing the employee's name, age, department, and salary.
- `give_raise`: Takes a percentage as an argument and increases the employee's salary by that percentage.
- `promote`: Takes a new department name as an argument and updates the employee's department.

Your implementation should include docstrings for the class and its methods.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
# Create an employee
emp = Employee("John Doe", 30, "Engineering", 75000)

# Get employee details
print(emp.get_details())  # Output: Name: John Doe, Age: 30, Department: Engineering, Salary: $75,000.00

# Give a 10% raise
emp.give_raise(10)
print(emp.get_details())  # Output: Name: John Doe, Age: 30, Department: Engineering, Salary: $82,500.00

# Promote to a new department
emp.promote("Management")
print(emp.get_details())  # Output: Name: John Doe, Age: 30, Department: Management, Salary: $82,500.00
